In Fig. 5, in state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_1$$\end{document}s1, agent a is uncertain about the ToM order of agent b. That is, she does not know whether agent b is confused and believes that agent a herself believes in contradictions. In epistemic logic, ‘not knowing whether φ φ’ is usually expressed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lnot K\varphi \wedge \lnot K\lnot \varphi $$\end{document}¬Kφ∧¬K¬φ, so agent a’s uncertainty about b’s confusion can be expressed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lnot B_aB_bB_a\bot \ \wedge \ \lnot B_a\lnot B_bB_a\bot $$\end{document}¬BaBbBa⊥∧¬Ba¬BbBa⊥. This formula is read as ‘agent a does not know whether b believes that a believes in the contradiction,’ and it is true in state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_1$$\end{document}s1.

Adding actions to logics of theory of mind

In the present section we introduce action models to ToMe. The resulting logic we will call ToMea, where the a signifies actions. Adding actions to ToM or ToM45 yields ToMa and ToM45a, respectively. Our methods are analogous to the ones employed in van Ditmarsch et al. (2007) and van Ditmarsch (2014a), with the crucial difference that our system adds theory of mind (ToM) orders as well as ToM limitations. Where possible, we employ the phrasing used in the aforementioned two works.

Action models

Our action models are based on the work of van Ditmarsch et al. (2007) and are defined as follows. Note that our action models are not S5, as we use beliefs, and not knowledge.

Definition 11

(Action Models) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}$$\end{document}L be any logical language for given parameters agents A, atoms P, and ToM orders L. An action model \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M}$$\end{document}M is a structure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{S}, \textbf{R}, \textbf{pre})$$\end{document}(S,R,pre) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{S}$$\end{document}S is a domain of action points, such that for each i∈ Ai∈A, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{R}(i)$$\end{document}R(i) is a relation on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{S}$$\end{document}S, and such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{pre} : (\textbf{S}\times A\times L) \rightarrow \mathcal {L}$$\end{document}pre:(S×A×L)→L is a preconditions function that assigns a precondition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{pre}(\textbf{s}) \in \mathcal {L}$$\end{document}pre(s)∈L to each combination of an action point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{s}\in \textbf{S}$$\end{document}s∈S, an agent i∈ Ai∈A, and a ToM order l∈ Ll∈L. A pointed action model is a structure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{M},\textbf{s})$$\end{document}(M,s) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{s}\in \textbf{S}$$\end{document}s∈S. We may omit the parentheses when no ambiguity can arise.

Note here that relations in action models, unlike those in our ToM models, do not have ToM orders associated with them. While including them might increase the system’s expressive power, we opt to omit them to keep the system closer to existing systems with action models and therefore easier to understand. Instead, we modify the semantics of action models in order to handle ToM orders. Action models such that for i∈ Ai∈A and l∈ Ll∈L, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{R}(i,l)$$\end{document}R(i,l) is a relation on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{S}$$\end{document}S, are left as an avenue for future work. For such a system, our soundness and strong completeness proofs (Theorem 2 and Theorem 3) still hold under the perturbation of replacing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{R}(i)$$\end{document}R(i) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{R}(i,l)$$\end{document}R(i,l).2

Unlike in the usual treatment of action models, we assign a precondition to each action point for each agent and each ToM order. This allows us to let agents at different ToM orders perceive the precondition of the same action point in a different manner. For example, when announcing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_bB_ap$$\end{document}BbBap, agent a with second- or higher-order ToM may perceive the announcement as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_bB_ap$$\end{document}BbBap, as she can do the two perspective switches required to understand the formula (from a to b, then back from b to a). For the same action point, we can assign ⊤ ⊤ as precondition for agent a at ToM orders zero and one, effectively making her ‘ignore’ the precondition if she does not have the ToM order required to understand \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_bB_ap$$\end{document}BbBap. Alternatively, we could follow Minculescu (2024) by having agents cut belief operators from formulas that they do not understand. In this case, agent a at first- and zero-order ToM would ‘cut’ the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_b$$\end{document}Bb operator and use precondition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_ap$$\end{document}Bap instead. This corresponds to the finding that children who fail to use second-order ToM tend to revert to first-order ToM use, instead of choosing randomly (Arslan et al., 2017b).

Language

To include action models, we modify the syntax presented in Definition 1. Our language is based on van Ditmarsch et al. (2007).

Definition 12

(Language of the logic of ToM with action models) Let A be a finite set of agents, P a countable set of propositional atoms, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L {:}{=} \{i\in \mathbb {N}_0\,|\,i\le \text {Max}\}$$\end{document}L:={i∈N0|i≤Max} for fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Max}\in \mathbb {N}_0$$\end{document}Max∈N0 (with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\in \mathbb {N}_0$$\end{document}0∈N0). The language of the logic of ToM with action models comprises the formulas \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \in \mathcal {L}_{\text {ToMa}}(A, P, \text {Max})$$\end{document}φ∈LToMa(A,P,Max), defined as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{array}{lll} \mathcal {L}_{\text {ToMa}}(A, P, \text {Max}) \ni \varphi & {:}{:}{=} & \ p\ |\ \lnot \varphi \ |\ (\varphi \wedge \varphi )\ |\ B_i^l\varphi \ |\ [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\varphi \\ \end{array} $$\end{document}LToMa(A,P,Max)∋φ::=p|¬φ|(φ∧φ)|Bilφ|[M,s]φwith i ∈ Ai∈A, p ∈ Pp∈P, l ∈ Ll∈L, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{M},\textbf{s})$$\end{document}(M,s) a pointed action model with finite domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{S}$$\end{document}S such that for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{t}\in \textbf{S}$$\end{document}t∈S, the precondition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{pre}(\textbf{t})$$\end{document}pre(t) is a finite \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_{\text {ToMa}}(A, P, \text {Max})$$\end{document}LToMa(A,P,Max)-formula that has already been constructed in a previous step of the inductive definition of the language.

By abbreviation, we define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\alpha \cup \beta ]\varphi = [\alpha ]\varphi \wedge [\beta ]\varphi $$\end{document}[α∪β]φ=[α]φ∧[β]φ (non-deterministic choice) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i\varphi = \bigwedge _{l\in L}B_i^l\varphi $$\end{document}Biφ=⋀l∈LBilφ (belief over all ToM orders of an agent). The usual abbreviations are used for ∨ ∨, → →, ↔ ↔, ⊥ ⊥, and ⊤ ⊤. Semantics

Our semantics uses the notation of van Ditmarsch et al. (2007). However, we depart from van Ditmarsch et al. (2007) and other semantics of action models by removing relations instead of removing states, which is more akin to arrow update logic (Kooi & Renne, 2011). Furthermore, our new precondition function includes agents and ToM orders as well.

Definition 13

(Semantics of the logic of ToM with actions) Given i∈ Ai∈A, p∈ Pp∈P, and l∈ Ll∈L, let M = (S,R,V)M=(S,R,V) be a ToMe model, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M} = (\textbf{S},\textbf{R},\textbf{pre})$$\end{document}M=(S,R,pre) be an action model. Our semantics is as in Definition 3 with the following addition:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{array}{lll} M,s\models [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\varphi & \text {iff}& (M\otimes \textbf{M}, (s,\textbf{s}))\models \varphi \\ \end{array}$$\end{document}M,s⊧[M,s]φiff(M⊗M,(s,s))⊧φIn the interpretation of action models, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M'=(M\otimes \textbf{M})$$\end{document}M′=(M⊗M) is a restricted modal product of an epistemic model and an action model, defined as M'=(S',R',V')M′=(S′,R′,V′) with

Note that we do not remove any states. It is possible that no agent considers the actual state to be possible: For example, when both agents use ToM-1, both agents only consider it possible that the other agent uses ToM-0, so the true state, where both agents use ToM-1, is not considered possible by either agent.3

Two examples of actions in models with theory of mind

In the present section, we show some examples of applying action models to epistemic models within our system.

An example of deception in a model with theory of mind

In this section, we show an example of applying a deceptive action to a model in our system. Consider once again the epistemic state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M, t_1$$\end{document}M,t1 shown in Fig. 3 in Section 2.7.1.

We resolve an action that models agent a lying to agent b that she does not believe that p. This action is based on those in van Ditmarsch (2014a). Agent b believes a is speaking truthfully. The resulting model after applying the action where agent a lies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lnot B_ap$$\end{document}¬Bap, to the model where a knows that p and b does not. Agent a (still) uses ToM-1, and agent b (still) uses ToM-0. The actual state has been underlined.

As seen in Fig. 7, after agent a lied to agent b that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lnot B_ap$$\end{document}¬Bap, agent b only considers states \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_1,\textbf{np})$$\end{document}(u1,np) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_2,\textbf{np})$$\end{document}(u2,np) to be possible. She cannot distinguish between these states, so she did not learn whether p, as her ToM order is too low to understand the lie. Note here that agent a does not consider states \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t_1,\textbf{np})$$\end{document}(t1,np) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t_2,\textbf{p})$$\end{document}(t2,p) to be possible. The state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t_1,\textbf{np})$$\end{document}(t1,np) corresponds to a state where p is true, yet agent a truthfully states that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lnot B_a p$$\end{document}¬Bap. State \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t_2,\textbf{p})$$\end{document}(t2,p) corresponds to a state where p is false, yet agent a lies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lnot B_a p$$\end{document}¬Bap. As seen in Fig. 3, agent a already knows that p in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_1$$\end{document}t1, so she cannot truthfully state that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lnot B_a p$$\end{document}¬Bap in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_1$$\end{document}t1. Similarly, in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_2$$\end{document}t2 she already knows that ¬ p¬p, so she cannot lie that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lnot B_a p$$\end{document}¬Bap.

We can also model how agent a lies that she believes that p is false. This is done by replacing the precondition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{np}$$\end{document}np with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_a\lnot p$$\end{document}Ba¬p. The resulting model is also the model found in Fig. 7.

In this particular example, we can remove states \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t_1,\textbf{np})$$\end{document}(t1,np), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_1,\textbf{p})$$\end{document}(u1,p), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_2,\textbf{p})$$\end{document}(u2,p), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t_2,\textbf{p})$$\end{document}(t2,p) without affecting the truth value of formulas at state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t_1,\textbf{p})$$\end{document}(t1,p). None of those states have incoming relations, so they cannot be reached using belief operators. However, if we initially used epistemic state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M, u_1$$\end{document}M,u1 instead of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M, t_1$$\end{document}M,t1, then the update would result in epistemic state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M', (u_1,\textbf{p})$$\end{document}M′,(u1,p). We cannot remove states simply because there are no incoming relations. A good example of this is the epistemic state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M, s_1$$\end{document}M,s1 with M = (S,R,V)M=(S,R,V) where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S=\{s_1,s_2,s_3\}$$\end{document}S={s1,s2,s3}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R(a,0) = \{(s_1,s_2),(s_2,s_2)\}$$\end{document}R(a,0)={(s1,s2),(s2,s2)}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R(b,0) = \{(s_1,s_3),(s_3,s_3)\}$$\end{document}R(b,0)={(s1,s3),(s3,s3)}. In this example, both agents a and b use ToM-0, and neither agent considers state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_1$$\end{document}s1 to be possible.

An example of actions and decreasing theory of mind orders

We now show an example of applying a public announcement to a model with decreasing ToM orders. Consider once again the epistemic state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M,s_1$$\end{document}M,s1 shown in Fig. 5 of Section 2.7.2.

We resolve an action that models agent b truthfully and publicly announcing that she considers it possible that agent a considers it possible that ⊤ ⊤, which corresponds to the formula \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{B}_b\hat{B}_a\top $$\end{document}B^bB^a⊤ being announced. A public announcement is a special single-action action model with a reflexive relation for all agents. Here, the precondition for the only action point is the formula that is being announced. In this example, we follow the methods of Minculescu (2024) by having agents cut belief operators, left to right, from announcements they do not understand. For agent a, the announcement \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{B}_b\hat{B}_a\top $$\end{document}B^bB^a⊤ requires two perspective switches (from a to b and then back to a), whereas for agent b, it only requires one perspective switch. The action model is a pointed action \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{M},\textbf{p})$$\end{document}(M,p) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M} = (\textbf{S},\textbf{R},\textbf{pre})$$\end{document}M=(S,R,pre) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{p}\in \textbf{S}$$\end{document}p∈S. It has action points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{S}=\{\textbf{p}\}$$\end{document}S={p}, relations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{R}(a)=\textbf{R}(b)=\{\textbf{p},\textbf{p}\}$$\end{document}R(a)=R(b)={p,p}, and preconditions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{pre}(\textbf{p},a,2) = \textbf{pre}(\textbf{p},b,2) = \textbf{pre}(\textbf{p},b,1) = \hat{B}_b\hat{B}_a\top $$\end{document}pre(p,a,2)=pre(p,b,2)=pre(p,b,1)=B^bB^a⊤, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{pre}(\textbf{p},a,1) = \textbf{pre}(\textbf{p},a,0) = \hat{B}_a\top $$\end{document}pre(p,a,1)=pre(p,a,0)=B^a⊤, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{pre}(\textbf{p},b,0) = \top $$\end{document}pre(p,b,0)=⊤. Here, agents perceive the announcement as the formula obtained by cutting belief operators from left to right until it matches their ToM order. The corresponding action model can be found in Fig. 8.Fig. 8A pointed action where agent b truthfully and publicly announces ‘I consider it possible that agent a considers it possible that ⊤ ⊤’. Agents cut belief operators from left to right until they can understand the announcement given their ToM order.

A pointed action where agent b truthfully and publicly announces ‘I consider it possible that agent a considers it possible that ⊤ ⊤’. Agents cut belief operators from left to right until they can understand the announcement given their ToM order.

Applying the action model in Fig. 8 to the model in Fig. 5 yields an M',s'M′,s′ where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M' = M\otimes \textbf{M}=(S',R',V')$$\end{document}M′=M⊗M=(S′,R′,V′) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s' = (s_1,\textbf{p})$$\end{document}s′=(s1,p). The resulting set of states is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S' = S\times \textbf{S} = \{(s_1,\textbf{p}), (s_2,\textbf{p}), (t_1,\textbf{p}), (t_2,\textbf{p}), (u_1,\textbf{p})\}$$\end{document}S′=S×S={(s1,p),(s2,p),(t1,p),(t2,p),(u1,p)} and the valuation is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V'(p) = S\times \textbf{S}$$\end{document}V′(p)=S×S. In \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_2$$\end{document}s2, the announcement \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{B}_b\hat{B}_a\top $$\end{document}B^bB^a⊤ is false, so agent a, at ToM order 2, no longer considers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_2$$\end{document}s2 (now \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(s_2,\textbf{s})$$\end{document}(s2,s)) to be possible. All other relations stay the same. The resulting relations are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R'(a,2) = \{((s_1,\textbf{p}),(s_1,\textbf{p})),((s_2,\textbf{p}),(s_1,\textbf{p}))\}$$\end{document}R′(a,2)={((s1,p),(s1,p)),((s2,p),(s1,p))}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R'(a,0) = \{((t_1,\textbf{p}),(u_1,\textbf{p})),((u_1,\textbf{p}),(u_1,\textbf{p}))\}$$\end{document}R′(a,0)={((t1,p),(u1,p)),((u1,p),(u1,p))}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R'(b,1)=\{((s_1,\textbf{p}),(t_1,\textbf{p})),((t_1,\textbf{p}),(t_1,\textbf{p}))\}$$\end{document}R′(b,1)={((s1,p),(t1,p)),((t1,p),(t1,p))}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R'(b,0)=\{((s_2,\textbf{p}),(t_2,\textbf{p})),((t_2,\textbf{p}),(t_2,\textbf{p}))\}$$\end{document}R′(b,0)={((s2,p),(t2,p)),((t2,p),(t2,p))}. The resulting epistemic state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M',(s_1,\textbf{p})$$\end{document}M′,(s1,p) can be found in Fig. 9.Fig. 9The resulting model after applying the truthful public announcement of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{B}_b\hat{B}_a\top $$\end{document}B^bB^a⊤ to the model where a is uncertain about agent b’s ToM order. In the new model, agent a no longer considers state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_2$$\end{document}s2 (now \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(s_2,\textbf{p})$$\end{document}(s2,p)) to be possible.

The resulting model after applying the truthful public announcement of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{B}_b\hat{B}_a\top $$\end{document}B^bB^a⊤ to the model where a is uncertain about agent b’s ToM order. In the new model, agent a no longer considers state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_2$$\end{document}s2 (now \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(s_2,\textbf{p})$$\end{document}(s2,p)) to be possible.

In Fig. 9, it can be seen that agent a now believes that agent b believes that agent a considers it possible that ⊤ ⊤. That is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_aB_b\hat{B}_a\top $$\end{document}BaBbB^a⊤. Agent a is now certain that agent b is at least a first-order ToM user. Agent b announcing that she considers it possible that a considers it possible that ⊤ ⊤ indeed required b to make a first-order ToM attribution.

Note that we obtain the same result if we use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{pre}(\textbf{p},a,0) = \textbf{pre}(\textbf{p},a,1) = \top $$\end{document}pre(p,a,0)=pre(p,a,1)=⊤. In this particular example, cutting belief operators from preconditions whenever their ToM order exceeds that of the agents, yields the same result as replacing those preconditions with ⊤ ⊤.

Axioms for actions

To add actions to our previous axiom systems, we propose the following reduction axioms, which take inspiration from van Ditmarsch et al. (2007):

Definition 14

(Axioms of actions)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{array}{|l|l|l|} \hline \text {AM1}& \text {Atomic Permanence}& [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]p\leftrightarrow p\\ \hline \text {AM2}& \text {Action and Negation}& [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\lnot \varphi \leftrightarrow \lnot [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\varphi \\ \hline \text {AM3}& \text {Action and Conjunction}& [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}](\varphi \wedge \psi )\leftrightarrow ([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\varphi \wedge [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\psi )\\ \hline \text {AM4}& \text {Action and Beliefs}& [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]B_i^l\varphi \leftrightarrow \bigwedge _{(\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)}B_i^l(\textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)\rightarrow [\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\varphi )\\ \hline \text {AM5}& \text {Action Composition}& [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}][\mathbf {M'},\textbf{s}']\varphi \leftrightarrow [\textbf{M},\textbf{s};\textbf{M}',\textbf{s}']\varphi \\ \hline \end{array}$$\end{document}AM1Atomic Permanence[M,s]p↔pAM2Action and Negation[M,s]¬φ↔¬[M,s]φAM3Action and Conjunction[M,s](φ∧ψ)↔([M,s]φ∧[M,s]ψ)AM4Action and Beliefs[M,s]Bilφ↔⋀(s,t)∈R(i)Bil(pre(t,i,l)→[M,t]φ)AM5Action Composition[M,s][M′,s′]φ↔[M,s;M′,s′]φ

Axiom systems ToMea, ToMa, and ToM45a are obtained by adding our action axioms to the systems ToMe, ToM, and ToM45, respectively. For axiom AM5, we must define action composition:

Definition 15

(Action Composition) - Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M} = (\textbf{S},\textbf{R},\textbf{pre})$$\end{document}M=(S,R,pre) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M}' = (\textbf{S}',\textbf{R}',\textbf{pre}')$$\end{document}M′=(S′,R′,pre′). Their composition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M};\textbf{M}'$$\end{document}M;M′ is the action model \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{S}'',\textbf{R}'',\textbf{pre}'')$$\end{document}(S′′,R′′,pre′′) such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \begin{array}{lll} \textbf{S}''& =& \textbf{S}\times \textbf{S}'\\ ((\textbf{s},\textbf{s}'),(\textbf{t},\textbf{t}'))\in \textbf{R}''(i)& \text {iff}& (\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)\ and\ (\textbf{s}',\textbf{t}')\in \textbf{R}'(i)\\ \textbf{pre}''((\textbf{s},\textbf{s}'),i,l)& =& \textbf{pre}(\textbf{s},i,l)\wedge [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\textbf{pre}'(\textbf{s}',i,l).\\ \end{array} $$\end{document}S′′=S×S′((s,s′),(t,t′))∈R′′(i)iff(s,t)∈R(i)and(s′,t′)∈R′(i)pre′′((s,s′),i,l)=pre(s,i,l)∧[M,s]pre′(s′,i,l).

Axiom AM4 is somewhat involved, so consider a simple example. Take the formula \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\textbf{M},\mathbf {s_1}]B_a^0p$$\end{document}[M,s1]Ba0p with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M} = (\textbf{S},\textbf{R},\textbf{pre})$$\end{document}M=(S,R,pre) where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{S}=\{\mathbf {s_1},\mathbf {s_2}\}$$\end{document}S={s1,s2}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{R}(a)=\{(\mathbf {s_1},\mathbf {s_1}),(\mathbf {s_1},\mathbf {s_2})\}$$\end{document}R(a)={(s1,s1),(s1,s2)}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{pre}(\mathbf {s_1},a,0) = p$$\end{document}pre(s1,a,0)=p, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{pre}(\mathbf {s_2},a,0) = B_b p$$\end{document}pre(s2,a,0)=Bbp. Axiom AM4 states this formula is equivalent to a conjunction over all action points that agent a considers possible from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {s_1}$$\end{document}s1. In our example, this is the formula \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_a^0(\textbf{pre}(\mathbf {s_1},a,0)\rightarrow [\textbf{M},\mathbf {s_1}]p)\wedge B_a^0(\textbf{pre}(\mathbf {s_2},a,0)\rightarrow [\textbf{M},\mathbf {s_2}]p)$$\end{document}Ba0(pre(s1,a,0)→[M,s1]p)∧Ba0(pre(s2,a,0)→[M,s2]p), which is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_a^0(p\rightarrow [\textbf{M},\mathbf {s_1}]p)\wedge B_a^0(B_b p\rightarrow [\textbf{M},\mathbf {s_2}]p)$$\end{document}Ba0(p→[M,s1]p)∧Ba0(Bbp→[M,s2]p).

Soundness of system ToMea

We now show soundness and strong completeness for ToMea, from which soundness and strong completeness of ToMa and ToM45a can readily be constructed. Once again, we allow for possibly infinite sets of premises Δ Δ. First, we show soundness of axioms AM4 and AM5 in Lemma 3 and Lemma 4, respectively. Afterwards, we show soundness of system ToMea in Theorem 2.

Lemma 3

Axiom AM4 is valid. That is,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{array}{c}\models [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]B_i^l\varphi \leftrightarrow \bigwedge _{(\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)}B_i^l(\textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)\rightarrow [\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\varphi )\end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⊧[M,s]Bilφ↔⋀(s,t)∈R(i)Bil(pre(t,i,l)→[M,t]φ)

Proof

First, noting that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\varphi \Leftrightarrow \lnot [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\lnot \varphi $$\end{document}[M,s]φ⇔¬[M,s]¬φ, we rewrite the formula to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{array}{c}\models [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\hat{B}_i^l\varphi \leftrightarrow \bigvee _{(\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)}\hat{B}_i^l(\textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)\wedge [\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\varphi )\end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⊧[M,s]B^ilφ↔⋁(s,t)∈R(i)B^il(pre(t,i,l)∧[M,t]φ)by taking the dual of both sides of the biconditional. Next, we prove both directions of the biconditional separately. Assume an arbitrary M, s.

Left to right. Assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M,s\models [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\hat{B}_i^l\varphi $$\end{document}M,s⊧[M,s]B^ilφ. Then, by definition of actions, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(M\otimes \textbf{M},(s,\textbf{s}))\models \hat{B}_i^l\varphi .$$\end{document}(M⊗M,(s,s))⊧B^ilφ. We use M'=(S',R',V')M′=(S′,R′,V′) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M\otimes \textbf{M}$$\end{document}M⊗M. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{B}_i^l\varphi $$\end{document}B^ilφ holds at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(s,\textbf{s})$$\end{document}(s,s) there must be a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t,\textbf{t})\in S'$$\end{document}(t,t)∈S′ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((s,\textbf{s}),(t,\textbf{t}))\in R'(i,l)$$\end{document}((s,s),(t,t))∈R′(i,l) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(M\otimes \textbf{M},(t,\textbf{t}))\models \varphi $$\end{document}(M⊗M,(t,t))⊧φ. From the latter, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M,t\models [\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\varphi $$\end{document}M,t⊧[M,t]φ. As we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((s,\textbf{s}),(t,\textbf{t}))\in R'(i,l)$$\end{document}((s,s),(t,t))∈R′(i,l) it must be the case, from the definition of R'R′, that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)$$\end{document}(s,t)∈R(i), (s,t)∈ R(i,l)(s,t)∈R(i,l), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M,t\models \textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)$$\end{document}M,t⊧pre(t,i,l). Then also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M,t\models \textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)\wedge [\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\varphi $$\end{document}M,t⊧pre(t,i,l)∧[M,t]φ, and from (s,t)∈ R(i,l)(s,t)∈R(i,l) it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M,s\models \hat{B}_i^l(\textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)\wedge [\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\varphi )$$\end{document}M,s⊧B^il(pre(t,i,l)∧[M,t]φ), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{t}$$\end{document}t is just some action point with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)$$\end{document}(s,t)∈R(i). From this it immediately follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M,s\models \bigvee _{(\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)} \hat{B}_i^l(\textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)\wedge [\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\varphi )$$\end{document}M,s⊧⋁(s,t)∈R(i)B^il(pre(t,i,l)∧[M,t]φ), which is what we set out to show.

Right to left. Assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M,s\models \bigvee _{(\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)}\hat{B}_i^l(\textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)\wedge [\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\varphi )$$\end{document}M,s⊧⋁(s,t)∈R(i)B^il(pre(t,i,l)∧[M,t]φ). Then there is some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{t}$$\end{document}t with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)$$\end{document}(s,t)∈R(i) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M,s\models \hat{B}_i^l(\textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)\wedge [\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\varphi )$$\end{document}M,s⊧B^il(pre(t,i,l)∧[M,t]φ). From this it follows that there is a t∈ St∈S such that (s,t)∈ R(i,l)(s,t)∈R(i,l) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M,t\models \textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)$$\end{document}M,t⊧pre(t,i,l) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M,t\models [\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\varphi $$\end{document}M,t⊧[M,t]φ. From the latter it holds that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(M\otimes \textbf{M},(t,\textbf{t}))\models \varphi $$\end{document}(M⊗M,(t,t))⊧φ. From (s,t)∈ R(i,l)(s,t)∈R(i,l), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)$$\end{document}(s,t)∈R(i), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M,t\models \textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)$$\end{document}M,t⊧pre(t,i,l), it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((s,\textbf{s}),(t,\textbf{t}))\in R'(i,l)$$\end{document}((s,s),(t,t))∈R′(i,l) (here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M\otimes \textbf{M} = (S',R',V')$$\end{document}M⊗M=(S′,R′,V′)). Given that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(M\otimes \textbf{M},(t,\textbf{t}))\models \varphi $$\end{document}(M⊗M,(t,t))⊧φ, we now have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(M\otimes \textbf{M},(s,\textbf{s}))\models \hat{B}_i^l\varphi $$\end{document}(M⊗M,(s,s))⊧B^ilφ, from which it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M,s\models [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\hat{B}_i^l\varphi $$\end{document}M,s⊧[M,s]B^ilφ, which is exactly what we set out to show.

Conclusion. Combining the right-to-left and left-to-right directions we obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\models [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\hat{B}_i^l\varphi \leftrightarrow \bigvee _{(\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)}\hat{B}_i^l(\textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)\wedge [\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\varphi )$$\end{document}⊧[M,s]B^ilφ↔⋁(s,t)∈R(i)B^il(pre(t,i,l)∧[M,t]φ), from which it immediately follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\models [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]B_i^l\varphi \leftrightarrow \bigwedge _{(\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)}B_i^l(\textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)\rightarrow [\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\varphi )$$\end{document}⊧[M,s]Bilφ↔⋀(s,t)∈R(i)Bil(pre(t,i,l)→[M,t]φ). □ □

Next, we show that axiom AM5 is valid.

Lemma 4

Axiom AM5 is valid. That is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\models [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}][\textbf{M}',\textbf{s}']\varphi \leftrightarrow [\textbf{M},\textbf{s};\textbf{M}',\textbf{s}']\varphi $$\end{document}⊧[M,s][M′,s′]φ↔[M,s;M′,s′]φ.

Proof

Take two arbitrary actions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{M},\textbf{s})$$\end{document}(M,s) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{M}',\textbf{s}')$$\end{document}(M′,s′), an arbitrary formula φ φ, and an arbitrary epistemic state M, s. We must show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\textbf{M},\textbf{s}][\textbf{M}',\textbf{s}']\varphi $$\end{document}[M,s][M′,s′]φ is equivalent to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\textbf{M},\textbf{s};\textbf{M}',\textbf{s}']\varphi $$\end{document}[M,s;M′,s′]φ. We do this by showing that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((M\otimes \textbf{M})\otimes \textbf{M}')$$\end{document}((M⊗M)⊗M′) is isomorphic to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(M\otimes (\textbf{M};\textbf{M}'))$$\end{document}(M⊗(M;M′)). The states, valuation, and relations are covered separately. For the remainder of this proof, we define the domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}(M_a)$$\end{document}D(Ma) of a model \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_a=(S_a,R_a,V_a)$$\end{document}Ma=(Sa,Ra,Va) to be its set of states \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_a$$\end{document}Sa, and similarly for action models.

States - Suppose we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(s,(\textbf{s},\textbf{s}'))\in \mathcal {D}(M\otimes (\textbf{M};\textbf{M}'))$$\end{document}(s,(s,s′))∈D(M⊗(M;M′)). Then also s∈ Ss∈S and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{s},\textbf{s}')\in \mathcal {D}(\textbf{M};\textbf{M}')$$\end{document}(s,s′)∈D(M;M′). From the latter we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{s}\in \textbf{S}$$\end{document}s∈S and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {s'}\in \textbf{S}'$$\end{document}s′∈S′. Now we obtain that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(s,\textbf{s})\in \mathcal {D}(M\otimes \textbf{M})$$\end{document}(s,s)∈D(M⊗M), and from there we obtain that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((s,\textbf{s}),\textbf{s}')\in \mathcal {D}((M\otimes \textbf{M})\otimes \textbf{M}')$$\end{document}((s,s),s′)∈D((M⊗M)⊗M′). Running the argument in reverse yields the other direction.

Valuation - Analogous to states.

Relations - Take any t∈ St∈S, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{t}\in \textbf{S}$$\end{document}t∈S, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{t}'\in \textbf{S}'$$\end{document}t′∈S′. We must show: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((s,(\textbf{s},\textbf{s}')),(t,(\textbf{t},\textbf{t}')))\in R_{M\otimes (\textbf{M};\textbf{M}')}(i,l)\Leftrightarrow (((s,\textbf{s}),\textbf{s}'),((t,\textbf{t}),\textbf{t}'))\in R_{(M\otimes \textbf{M})\otimes \textbf{M}'}(i,l)$$\end{document}((s,(s,s′)),(t,(t,t′)))∈RM⊗(M;M′)(i,l)⇔(((s,s),s′),((t,t),t′))∈R(M⊗M)⊗M′(i,l). We show the right-to-left direction, leaving left-to-right to the reader.

Right to left - For readability, we number our proof steps.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \begin{array}{llll} (1)& \text {Suppose }& (((s,\textbf{s}),\textbf{s}'),((t,\textbf{t}),\textbf{t}'))\in R_{(M\otimes \textbf{M})\otimes \textbf{M}'}(i,l).& \\ (2)& \text {Then also }& ((s,\textbf{s}),(t,\textbf{t}))\in R_{(M\otimes \textbf{M})}(i,l),& (\text {from }(1))\\ (3)& & (\textbf{s}',\textbf{t}')\in \textbf{R}'(i),& (\text {from }(1))\\ (4)& \text {and}& M\otimes \textbf{M}, (t,\textbf{t})\models \text {and }& M,t\models \textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l).& (\text {from }(2))\\ (8)& \text {Next, we know}& M,t\models [\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\textbf{pre}'(\textbf{t}',i,l),& (\text {from (4)})\\ (9)& $$\end{document}(1)Suppose(((s,s),s′),((t,t),t′))∈R(M⊗M)⊗M′(i,l).(2)Then Conclusion - By proving isomorphism for the states, valuation, and relations, we have shown that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((M\otimes \textbf{M})\otimes \textbf{M}')$$\end{document}((M⊗M)⊗M′) is isomorphic to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(M\otimes (\textbf{M};\textbf{M}'))$$\end{document}(M⊗(M;M′)), and therefore that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\models [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}][\textbf{M}',\textbf{s}']\varphi \leftrightarrow [\textbf{M},\textbf{s};\textbf{M}',\textbf{s}']\varphi $$\end{document}⊧[M,s][M′,s′]φ↔[M,s;M′,s′]φ. □ □

Finally, we show soundness of ToMea.

Theorem 2

Axiom system ToMea is sound with respect to its semantics.

Proof

To show soundness of ToMea, we show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \vdash \varphi \Rightarrow \Delta \models \varphi $$\end{document}Δ⊢φ⇒Δ⊧φ for all (possibly infinite) sets of premises Δ Δ. We have soundness of ToMe, so we only show soundness of axioms AM1 through AM5. Above, we have shown soundness of AM4 and AM5 in Lemma 3 and Lemma 4, respectively. Soundness of the other axioms is left to the reader: Soundness of AM1, AM2, and AM3 can be proven either directly or in very few steps from our semantic definitions. Having shown soundness for ToMe with Theorem 1, as well as soundness for axioms AM1 through AM5, we conclude, through an inductive proof over the derivations in ToMea, that the axiom system of ToMea is sound with respect to its semantics. □ □

Strong completeness of system ToMea

To show strong completeness of ToMea we use a proof by translation, a standard method when showing completeness of dynamic logics (Kooi, 2007; van Ditmarsch et al., 2007; Baltag et al., 2023). We define a translation function t(φ )t(φ) that translates any formula φ φ into an equivalent one that does not use any action operators, and then proceed to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash \varphi \leftrightarrow t(\varphi )$$\end{document}⊢φ↔t(φ), and hence also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \vdash \varphi \leftrightarrow t(\varphi )$$\end{document}Δ⊢φ↔t(φ) for all (possibly infinite) sets of premises Δ Δ. To be able to use a proof by induction for the latter, we define our own complexity measure c(φ )c(φ) such that the ordering of the complexity of formulas corresponds to the ordering inherent in our translation function. First, we define our translation function t(φ )t(φ), for all formulas φ φ of ToMea, as follows:

Definition 16

The translation function t(φ )t(φ) of formulas φ φ, inspired by van Ditmarsch et al. (2007), is defined as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \begin{array}{lll} t(p)& =& p\\ t(\lnot \varphi )& =& \lnot t(\varphi )\\ t(\varphi \wedge \psi )& =& t(\varphi )\wedge t(\psi )\\ t(B_i^l\varphi )& =& B_i^l t(\varphi )\\ t([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]p)& =& t(p)\\ t([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\lnot \varphi )& =& t(\lnot [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\varphi )\\ t([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}](\varphi \wedge \psi ))& =& t([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\varphi )\wedge ([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\psi )\\ t([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}] B_i^l\varphi )& =& t(\bigwedge _{(\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)}B_i^l(\textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)\rightarrow [\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\varphi ))\\ t([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}][\mathbf {M'},\textbf{s}']\varphi )& =& t([\textbf{M},\textbf{s};\textbf{M}',\textbf{s}']\varphi )\\ \end{array} $$\end{document}t(p)=pt(¬φ)=¬t(φ)t(φ∧ψ)=t(φ)∧t(ψ)t(Bilφ)=Bilt(φ)t([M,s]p)=t(p)t([M,s]¬φ)=t(¬[M,s]φ)t([M,s](φ∧ψ))=t([M,s]φ)∧([M,s]ψ)t([M,s]Bilφ)=t(⋀(s,t)∈R(i)Bil(pre(t,i,l)→[M,t]φ))t([M,s][M′,s′]φ)=t([M,s;M′,s′]φ)

As is usual in such translation functions, in the formulas on the right-hand side, the action operator has been pushed inwards. Furthermore, our translation corresponds exactly to our axiom system. Next, we define the complexity measure we use in our inductive proof of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash \varphi \leftrightarrow t(\varphi )$$\end{document}⊢φ↔t(φ), which is inspired by van Ditmarsch et al. (2007)4.

Definition 17

We may omit ‘\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in A, l\in L$$\end{document}i∈A,l∈L’ in the complexity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M}$$\end{document}M. Lemma 5 is proven by proving each property separately. We show proofs for items 5 and 6, where the other items are handled akin to the complexity of public announcement logic in van Ditmarsch et al.
