Proof of Lemma 5.5

We rewrite both sides of the inequality in turn.

Left-hand side. First, we rewrite the left-hand side using a series of equalities:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \begin{array}{ll} c([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}] B_i^l\varphi )& =\\ (3+c(\textbf{M}))\cdot c(B_i^l\varphi )& =\\ 3+3c(\varphi )+c(\textbf{M})+c(\varphi )c(\textbf{M})& \\ \end{array} $$\end{document}c([M,s]Bilφ)=(3+c(M))·c(Bilφ)=3+3c(φ)+c(M)+c(φ)c(M)Right-hand side. Now we rewrite the right-hand side:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \begin{array}{ll} c\left( \bigwedge _{(\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)}B_i^l(\textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)\rightarrow [\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\varphi )\right) & \\ & =^*\\ n + max\{c(B_i^l(\textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)\rightarrow [\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\varphi ))|(\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)\}& \\ & =^\dagger \\ n + max\{c(B_i^l\lnot (\lnot \lnot \textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)\wedge \lnot [\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\varphi ))|(\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)\}& \\ & =\\ 4 + n + max(\{c([\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\varphi )\}\cup \{1+c(\textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l))|(\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)\})& \\ & =^\ddagger \\ 4 + n + c([\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\varphi )& \\ & =\\ 4+n+3c(\varphi )+c(\varphi )c(\textbf{M})& \\ \end{array} $$\end{document}c⋀(s,t)∈R(i)Bil(pre(t,i,l)→[M,t]φ)=∗n+max{c(Bil(pre(t,i,l)→[M,t]φ))|(s,t)∈R(i)}=†n+max{c(Bil¬(¬¬pre(t,i,l)∧¬[M,t]φ))|(s,t)∈R(i)}=4+n+max({c([M,t]φ)}∪{1+c(pre(t,i,l))|(s,t)∈R(i)})=‡4+n+c([M,t]φ)=4+n+3c(φ)+c(φ)c(M)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phantom {a}^*$$\end{document}∗ For the outer conjunction, we add 1 for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)$$\end{document}(s,t)∈R(i), minus 1. We call this number n.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phantom {a}^\dagger $$\end{document}† In our syntax, A→ BA→B is an abbreviation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lnot A\vee B$$\end{document}¬A∨B, which itself is an abbreviation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lnot (\lnot \lnot A\wedge \lnot B)$$\end{document}¬(¬¬A∧¬B).

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phantom {a}^\ddagger $$\end{document}‡ Note that by its definition, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c([\textbf{M},\textbf{t}]\varphi )$$\end{document}c([M,t]φ) must be at least \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1+c(\textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l))$$\end{document}1+c(pre(t,i,l)), for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{t}\in \textbf{S}$$\end{document}t∈S, i∈ Ai∈A, and l∈ Ll∈L.

Comparison. From our previous rewrites, we now know we have to show that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \begin{array}{lll} 3+3c(\varphi )+c(\textbf{M})+c(\varphi )c(\textbf{M})& >& 4+n+3c(\varphi )+c(\varphi )c(\textbf{M}).\\ \end{array} $$\end{document}3+3c(φ)+c(M)+c(φ)c(M)>4+n+3c(φ)+c(φ)c(M).After subtracting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3 + 3c(\varphi ) + c(\varphi )c(\textbf{M})$$\end{document}3+3c(φ)+c(φ)c(M), it remains to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c(\textbf{M})>1 + n$$\end{document}c(M)>1+n, which is the same as showing that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\textbf{R}|+max\{c(\textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)|\textbf{t}\in \textbf{M})\}>1+n$$\end{document}|R|+max{c(pre(t,i,l)|t∈M)}>1+n. Recall that n is the number of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)$$\end{document}(s,t)∈R(i), minus one. At most, this is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\textbf{R}|-1$$\end{document}|R|-1. Therefore, it is sufficient to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\textbf{R}|+max\{c(\textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)|\textbf{t}\in \textbf{M})\}>|\textbf{R}|$$\end{document}|R|+max{c(pre(t,i,l)|t∈M)}>|R|. This holds, because preconditions have at least complexity 1, since preconditions cannot be formulas smaller than atoms. □ □

Next, we show that item 6 of the ordering of our complexity holds:

Proof of Lemma 5.5

We start by rewriting the left-hand side, where we skip any intermediate steps:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{array}{ll} c([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}][\mathbf {M'},\textbf{s}']\varphi )& =\\ 9c(\varphi )+3c(\varphi )c(\textbf{M}) + 3c(\varphi )c(\mathbf {M'})+ c(\varphi )c(\textbf{M})c(\mathbf {M'}).& \\ \end{array} $$\end{document}c([M,s][M′,s′]φ)=9c(φ)+3c(φ)c(M)+3c(φ)c(M′)+c(φ)c(M)c(M′).Abusing notation, we abbreviate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$max\{c(\textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l))|\textbf{t}\in \textbf{M}\}$$\end{document}max{c(pre(t,i,l))|t∈M} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$max(c(\textbf{pre}))$$\end{document}max(c(pre)), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$max\{c(\mathbf {pre'}(\mathbf {t'},i,l))|\mathbf {t'}\in \mathbf {M'}\}$$\end{document}max{c(pre′(t′,i,l))|t′∈M′} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$max(c(\mathbf {pre'}))$$\end{document}max(c(pre′)). We continue rewriting to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{array}{l}9c(\varphi )+3c(\varphi )\cdot |\textbf{R}|+3c(\varphi )\cdot max(c(\textbf{pre})) + 3c(\varphi )\cdot |\mathbf {R'}| + 3c(\varphi )\cdot max(c(\mathbf {pre'}))+\\ c(\varphi )\cdot \mathbf {|R|}\cdot \mathbf {|R'|} +c(\varphi )\cdot \mathbf {|\textbf{R}|}\cdot max(c(\mathbf {pre'}))+ c(\varphi )\cdot |\mathbf {R'}|\cdot max(c(\textbf{pre}))+\\ c(\varphi )\cdot max(c(\textbf{pre}))\cdot max(c(\mathbf {pre'})).\end{array}$$\end{document}9c(φ)+3c(φ)·|R|+3c(φ)·max(c(pre))+3c(φ)·|R′|+3c(φ)·max(c(pre′))+c(φ)·|R|·|R′|+c(φ)·|R|·max(c(pre′))+c(φ)·|R′|·max(c(pre))+c(φ)·max(c(pre))·max(c(pre′)).Recall from Definition 15 that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{pre}''((\textbf{s},\textbf{s}'))=\textbf{pre}(\textbf{s})\wedge [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\textbf{pre}'(\textbf{s}')$$\end{document}pre′′((s,s′))=pre(s)∧[M,s]pre′(s′). We now rewrite the right-hand side, where we abbreviate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M};\textbf{M}'$$\end{document}M;M′ as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M}''$$\end{document}M′′.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{array}{lr} c([\textbf{M},\textbf{s};\textbf{M}',\textbf{s}']\varphi )& =\\ (3+c(\textbf{M}''))\cdot c(\varphi )& =\\ (3+|\mathbf {R''}|+max\{c(\textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)\wedge [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\textbf{pre}'(\textbf{t}',i,l))|\textbf{t}\in \textbf{M}\text { and }\mathbf {t'}\in \mathbf {M'}\})\cdot c(\varphi )& =\\ (4+|\mathbf {R''}|+max\{(3+c(\textbf{M}))\cdot c(\textbf{pre}'(\textbf{t}',i,l))|\mathbf {t'}\in \mathbf {M'}\})\cdot c(\varphi )& =\\ {4c(\varphi )} + {c(\varphi )\cdot |\mathbf {R''}|} + {3c(\varphi )\cdot max(c(\mathbf {pre'}))} + {c(\varphi )\cdot |\textbf{R}|\cdot max(c(\mathbf {pre'}))} +& \\ {c(\varphi )\cdot \end{array}$$\end{document}c([M,s;M′,s′]φ)=(3+c(M′′))·c(φ)=(3+|R′′|+max{c(pre(t,i,l)∧[M,s]pre′(t′,i,l))|t∈Mandt′∈M′})·c(φ)=(4+|R′′|+max{(3+c(M))·c(pre′(t′,i,l))|t′∈M′})·c(φ)=4c(φ)+c(φ)·|R′′|+3c(φ)·max(c(pre′))+c(φ)·|R|·max(c(pre′))+c(φ)·max(c(pre))·max(c(pre′))Note that the terms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${3c(\varphi )\cdot max(c(\mathbf {pre'}))} + {c(\varphi )\cdot |\textbf{R}|\cdot max(c(\mathbf {pre'}))} + c(\varphi )\cdot max(c(\textbf{pre}))\cdot max(c(\mathbf {pre'}))$$\end{document}3c(φ)·max(c(pre′))+c(φ)·|R|·max(c(pre′))+c(φ)·max(c(pre))·max(c(pre′)) occur in both formulas. If we subtract these terms from the left-hand side, the left-hand side becomes\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{array}{l}9c(\varphi )+3c(\varphi )\cdot |\textbf{R}|+3c(\varphi )\cdot max(c(\textbf{pre})) + 3c(\varphi )\cdot |\mathbf {R'}| + \\ c(\varphi )\cdot \mathbf {|R|}\cdot \mathbf {|R'|} + c(\varphi )\cdot |\mathbf {R'}|\cdot max(c(\textbf{pre})).\\ \end{array}$$\end{document}9c(φ)+3c(φ)·|R|+3c(φ)·max(c(pre))+3c(φ)·|R′|+c(φ)·|R|·|R′|+c(φ)·|R′|·max(c(pre)).If we subtract the same terms from the right-hand side, then the right-hand side becomes\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 4c(\varphi ) + {c(\varphi )\cdot |\mathbf {R''}|.} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}4c(φ)+c(φ)·|R′′|.Therefore, for the left-hand side to be larger than the right-hand side, it is sufficient to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$9c(\varphi )> 4c(\varphi )$$\end{document}9c(φ)>4c(φ) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c(\varphi )\cdot |\textbf{R}|\cdot |\textbf{R}'|\ge c(\varphi )\cdot |\textbf{R}''|$$\end{document}c(φ)·|R|·|R′|≥c(φ)·|R′′|. First, note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$9c(\varphi )> 4c(\varphi )$$\end{document}9c(φ)>4c(φ) is immediate. Now we show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c(\varphi )\cdot \mathbf {|R|}\cdot \mathbf {|R'|} \ge c(\varphi )\cdot |\mathbf {R''}|$$\end{document}c(φ)·|R|·|R′|≥c(φ)·|R′′|. Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {|R|}\cdot \mathbf {|R'|}\ge |\mathbf {R''}|$$\end{document}|R|·|R′|≥|R′′| follows directly from the definition of action composition. From this it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}][\mathbf {M'},\textbf{s}']\varphi ) > c([\textbf{M},\textbf{s};\textbf{M}',\textbf{s}']\varphi )$$\end{document}c([M,s][M′,s′]φ)>c([M,s;M′,s′]φ), which is what we set out to prove. □ □

Now we can proceed to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \vdash \varphi \leftrightarrow t(\varphi )$$\end{document}Δ⊢φ↔t(φ):

Lemma 6

For all formulas φ φ and all (possibly infinite) sets of premises Δ Δ, the following holds: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \vdash \varphi \leftrightarrow t(\varphi )$$\end{document}Δ⊢φ↔t(φ)

Proof

By induction on c(φ )c(φ), found in Definition 17, we show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash \varphi \leftrightarrow t(\varphi )$$\end{document}⊢φ↔t(φ), from which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \vdash \varphi \leftrightarrow t(\varphi )$$\end{document}Δ⊢φ↔t(φ) immediately follows. We follow the proofs in van Ditmarsch et al. (2007).

Base case - \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash p\leftrightarrow t(p)$$\end{document}⊢p↔t(p). Immediate from the definition of t(φ )t(φ).

Inductive hypothesis - Assume for all φ φ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c(\varphi )\le n$$\end{document}c(φ)≤n that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash \varphi \leftrightarrow t(\varphi )$$\end{document}⊢φ↔t(φ).

Inductive step - Assume an arbitrary φ φ with c(φ ) = n+1c(φ)=n+1. We proceed with one case for each operator in the language. For formulas \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\varphi $$\end{document}[M,s]φ we include an additional case for each operator in the language. We only show the cases for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi =\lnot \psi $$\end{document}φ=¬ψ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi =[\textbf{M},\textbf{s}](\psi _1\wedge \psi _2)$$\end{document}φ=[M,s](ψ1∧ψ2), leaving the rest to the reader.

Case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lnot \psi $$\end{document}¬ψ: Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c(\lnot \psi ) = n + 1$$\end{document}c(¬ψ)=n+1. Therefore, c(ψ ) = nc(ψ)=n, so we can apply the induction hypothesis to ψ ψ, that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash \psi \leftrightarrow t(\psi )$$\end{document}⊢ψ↔t(ψ). Then by contraposition also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash \lnot \psi \leftrightarrow \lnot t(\psi )$$\end{document}⊢¬ψ↔¬t(ψ), and by definition of the translation function also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash \lnot \psi \leftrightarrow t(\lnot \psi )$$\end{document}⊢¬ψ↔t(¬ψ), as required.

Case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\textbf{M},\textbf{s}](\psi _1\wedge \psi _2)$$\end{document}[M,s](ψ1∧ψ2): Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}](\psi _1\wedge \psi _2)) = n + 1$$\end{document}c([M,s](ψ1∧ψ2))=n+1. But we know from Lemma 5 that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}](\psi _1\wedge \psi _2))>c([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\psi _1\wedge [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\psi _2)$$\end{document}c([M,s](ψ1∧ψ2))>c([M,s]ψ1∧[M,s]ψ2), so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\psi _1\wedge [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\psi _2)\le n$$\end{document}c([M,s]ψ1∧[M,s]ψ2)≤n. Then the induction hypothesis must hold for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\psi _1\wedge [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\psi _2$$\end{document}[M,s]ψ1∧[M,s]ψ2. Therefore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash ([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\psi _1\wedge [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\psi _2)\leftrightarrow t([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\psi _1\wedge [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\psi _2)$$\end{document}⊢([M,s]ψ1∧[M,s]ψ2)↔t([M,s]ψ1∧[M,s]ψ2). Then, from the translation function, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash ([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\psi _1\wedge [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\psi _2)\leftrightarrow t([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}](\psi _1\wedge \psi _2))$$\end{document}⊢([M,s]ψ1∧[M,s]ψ2)↔t([M,s](ψ1∧ψ2)). But from axiom AM3 we also know that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}](\psi _1\wedge \psi _2)\leftrightarrow ([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\psi _1\wedge [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}]\psi _2)$$\end{document}⊢[M,s](ψ1∧ψ2)↔([M,s]ψ1∧[M,s]ψ2), so also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash [\textbf{M},\textbf{s}](\psi _1\wedge \psi _2)\leftrightarrow t([\textbf{M},\textbf{s}](\psi _1\wedge \psi _2))$$\end{document}⊢[M,s](ψ1∧ψ2)↔t([M,s](ψ1∧ψ2)), as required.

Conclusion - By induction on c(φ )c(φ) we have shown that for all formulas φ φ of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_{\text {ToMa}}$$\end{document}LToMa, it holds that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vdash \varphi \leftrightarrow t(\varphi )$$\end{document}⊢φ↔t(φ). From this it immediately follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \vdash \varphi \leftrightarrow t(\varphi )$$\end{document}Δ⊢φ↔t(φ). □ □

Now we are ready to show strong completeness:

Theorem 3

ToMea is strongly complete with respect to its semantics.

Proof

From the soundness of ToMea in Theorem 2, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \vdash \varphi \leftrightarrow t(\varphi )$$\end{document}Δ⊢φ↔t(φ), as shown in Lemma 6, it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \models \varphi \leftrightarrow t(\varphi )$$\end{document}Δ⊧φ↔t(φ), for all (possibly infinite) sets of premises Δ Δ. We will show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \models \varphi \Rightarrow \Delta \vdash \varphi $$\end{document}Δ⊧φ⇒Δ⊢φ. Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \models \varphi $$\end{document}Δ⊧φ. Then also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \models t(\varphi )$$\end{document}Δ⊧t(φ), as our axioms are sound. As t(φ )t(φ) does not contain any action operators, it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \vdash t(\varphi )$$\end{document}Δ⊢t(φ), by strong completeness of ToMe. From \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \vdash \varphi \leftrightarrow t(\varphi )$$\end{document}Δ⊢φ↔t(φ) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \vdash t(\varphi )$$\end{document}Δ⊢t(φ) it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \vdash \varphi $$\end{document}Δ⊢φ, concluding the proof. □ □

Strong completeness of ToMa and ToM45a follow from the strong completeness of ToMea and the strong completeness of systems ToM and ToM45 as shown in Section 2.6.

Preservation of properties

In the present section, we show that the properties Downward-ToM-Other, Downward-ToM-Self, ToM-Transitivity, and ToM-Euclidicity are preserved under action model update. The properties Downward-ToM-Other and Downward-ToM-Self are always preserved, regardless of the action model. ToM-Transitivity and ToM-Euclidicity are only preserved if the action model itself is transitive and euclidean, respectively.

In Lemma 7 respectively Lemma 8, we show preservation under update for ToM-Euclidicity and Downward-ToM-Other, leaving ToM-Transitivity and Downward-ToM-Self to the reader. Afterwards, we show in Theorem 4 that all our properties are preserved under certain conditions.

Lemma 7

For euclidean action models, ToM-Euclidicity is preserved under update.

Proof

Assume a ToM-euclidean model M and a euclidean action model \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M}$$\end{document}M. To show this we only need to show that “If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((s,\textbf{s}),(t,\textbf{t}))\in R_{M\otimes \textbf{M}}(i,l)$$\end{document}((s,s),(t,t))∈RM⊗M(i,l) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((s,\textbf{s}),(u,\textbf{u}))\in R_{M\otimes \textbf{M}}(i,m)$$\end{document}((s,s),(u,u))∈RM⊗M(i,m) with l≥ ml≥m, then also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((t,\textbf{t}),(u,\textbf{u}))\in R_{M\otimes \textbf{M}}(i,m)$$\end{document}((t,t),(u,u))∈RM⊗M(i,m)”.

So assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((s,\textbf{s}),(t,\textbf{t}))\in R_{M\otimes \textbf{M}}(i,l)$$\end{document}((s,s),(t,t))∈RM⊗M(i,l) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((s,\textbf{s}),(u,\textbf{u}))\in R_{M\otimes \textbf{M}}(i,m)$$\end{document}((s,s),(u,u))∈RM⊗M(i,m) with l≥ ml≥m. From the former, it follows that (s,t)∈ R(i,l)(s,t)∈R(i,l), that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{s},\textbf{t})\in \textbf{R}(i)$$\end{document}(s,t)∈R(i), and that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M,t\models \textbf{pre}(\textbf{t},i,l)$$\end{document}M,t⊧pre(t,i,l). From \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((s,\textbf{s}),(u,\textbf{u}))\in R_{M\otimes \textbf{M}}(i,m)$$\end{document}((s,s),(u,u))∈RM⊗M(i,m), it follows that (s,u)∈ R(i,m)(s,u)∈R(i,m), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{s},\textbf{u})\in \textbf{R}(i)$$\end{document}(s,u)∈R(i), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M,u\models \textbf{pre}(\textbf{u},i,m)$$\end{document}M,u⊧pre(u,i,m). Now, from the ToM-euclidicity of M, we have that (t,u)∈ R(i,m)(t,u)∈R(i,m), and from the euclidicity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M}$$\end{document}M, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{t},\textbf{u})\in \textbf{R}(i)$$\end{document}(t,u)∈R(i). From these two and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M,u\models \textbf{pre}(\textbf{u},i,m)$$\end{document}M,u⊧pre(u,i,m), it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((t,\textbf{t}),(u,\textbf{u}))\in R_{M\otimes \textbf{M}}(i,m)$$\end{document}((t,t),(u,u))∈RM⊗M(i,m), which is what we set out to show. □ □

Next, we show preservation for Downward-ToM-Other.

Lemma 8

Downward-ToM-Other is preserved under update.

Proof

Let M be a model with the property Downward-ToM-Other, and take an arbitrary action model \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M}$$\end{document}M. We need to show that “There are no \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((s,\textbf{s}), (t,\textbf{t})) \in R_{M\otimes \textbf{M}}(i, m)$$\end{document}((s,s),(t,t))∈RM⊗M(i,m) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((t,\textbf{t}), (u,\textbf{u})) \in R_{M\otimes \textbf{M}}(j, l)$$\end{document}((t,t),(u,u))∈RM⊗M(j,l) with i≠ ji≠j and l≥ ml≥m”.

To this end, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((s,\textbf{s}), (t,\textbf{t})) \in R_{M\otimes \textbf{M}}(i, m)$$\end{document}((s,s),(t,t))∈RM⊗M(i,m) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((t,\textbf{t}), (u,\textbf{u})) \in R_{M\otimes \textbf{M}}(j, l)$$\end{document}((t,t),(u,u))∈RM⊗M(j,l) be relations. To prove our claim, we now only have to show that i≠ ji≠j implies l<ml<m.

First, assume that i≠ ji≠j. From \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((s,\textbf{s}), (t,\textbf{t})) \in R_{M\otimes \textbf{M}}(i, m)$$\end{document}((s,s),(t,t))∈RM⊗M(i,m), it follows that (s,t)∈ R(i,m)(s,t)∈R(i,m), and from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((t,\textbf{t}), (u,\textbf{u})) \in R_{M\otimes \textbf{M}}(j, l)$$\end{document}((t,t),(u,u))∈RM⊗M(j,l), it follows that (t,u)∈ R(j,l)(t,u)∈R(j,l). From these two, from the assumption that i≠ ji≠j, and from model M having the property Downward-ToM-Other, it follows immediately that l<ml<m, as required. □ □

Now, we can show that, under the right circumstances, all our properties are preserved under update.

Theorem 4

For transitive and euclidean action models, all of the properties ToM-Transitive, ToM-Euclidicean, Downward-ToM-Other, and Downward-ToM-Self are preserved under update.

Proof

ToM-Euclidicity and Downward-ToM-Other are shown in Lemma 7 and Lemma 8, respectively. The proofs for ToM-Transitivity and Downward-ToM-Self are left to the reader. The proof for ToM-Transitivity is similar to that of ToM-Euclidicity, under the assumption that the action models are transitive. The proof for Downward-ToM-Self is near-identical to the proof for Downward-ToM-Other. □ □

An interesting property of Downward-ToM-Other and Downward-ToM-Self is that they are preserved independently of the structure of the action model. If a model has these properties, then no action can remove them.

Discussion

The present section discusses potential areas of future research. In Section 4.1, we discuss possible behavioural research. In Section 4.2, we consider how the logic can be improved. Next, we suggest potential uses for our logic in computational cognitive modelling in Section 4.3. Lastly, in Section 4.4, we conclude our work.

Behavioural research

In the development of our logic we make several assumptions about human theory of mind (ToM) reasoning, which lead to testable hypotheses:

In Section 2.3 we discuss why we do not employ special ToM atoms, unlike the logic in Arthaud and Rinard (2023). However, our justification is only an intuitive one, so we suggest that behavioural research be conducted to investigate the prevalence of reasoning about explicit ToM orders.

In Section 2.5.1 we explain that from the perspective of a ToM-l agent, ToM-m agents with m≥ lm≥l do not exist. We interpret such ToM-l agents as being ‘confused’, and believing that ToM-m agents believe in contradictions. However, the results of the child studies in Birch and Bloom (2003) and Ghrear et al. (2021) suggest that ToM-0 users may reason from their own perspective when answering questions about the beliefs of others, as opposed to attributing contradiction. We recommend that more behavioural experiments be conducted to investigate how adult participants reason about statements that are beyond their ToM capabilities.

A major difference between Arthaud and Rinard (2023)’s depth-bounded epistemic logic and our logic of theory of mind is that announcements in Arthaud and Rinard (2023) decrease an agent’s depth. For example, if an agent can use third-order ToM, it will become a zero-order agent after three announcements of ‘agent a believes that p.’ Meanwhile, actions in our logic do not decrease the ToM order of our accessibility relations, because children seem quite capable of processing multiple sequential ToM-1 (or higher) announcements despite only having ToM-1 themselves (Arslan et al., 2017b). To resolve this difference, future behavioural research can investigate whether adults’ ToM capabilities decrease when exposed to multiple sequential announcements about other agents’ beliefs.

Formal logic; Modeling choices

In the present paper we present a dynamic logic with ToM limitations that can be used to model deception. We decided to forego issues on decidability and model checking complexity, leaving these for future work instead. In Section 3.1, we define the preconditions of action models as a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{pre}:(S\times A\times L)\rightarrow \mathcal {L}$$\end{document}pre:(S×A×L)→L. In Section 3.4 we show two ways in which these preconditions can be used to have agents ‘ignore’ or ‘cut’ formulas. In ongoing work we extend our logic with a precise formalization of the depth of formulas, similar to Arthaud and Rinard (2023), as well as a formally defined cutting function based on the results of Minculescu (2024); Minculescu et al. (2025). It also remains an open question whether and how our action models can be translated into equivalent action models with precondition functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{pre}:S\rightarrow \mathcal {L}$$\end{document}pre:S→L or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{pre}:(S\times A)\rightarrow \mathcal {L}$$\end{document}pre:(S×A)→L.

If it turns out that humans do regularly reason about the exact ToM orders of others, then our logic needs to be extended with depth atoms. If needed, one can add ToM atoms to our logic by adding special depth atoms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_i^l$$\end{document}til to the language and semantics, using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M,s\models t_i^l$$\end{document}M,s⊧til iff \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\in V(t_i^l)$$\end{document}s∈V(til), and including novel axioms and model properties. The axiom \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lnot (t_i^l\wedge \lnot B_i^m\bot )$$\end{document}¬(til∧¬Bim⊥) for m>lm>l prevents agents from using an order higher than the one they have. A unique depth axiom, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lnot (t_i^l\wedge t_i^m)$$\end{document}¬(til∧tim) for l≠ ml≠m, taken from Arthaud and Rinard (2023), ensures agents only have one ToM order. The axiom \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bigvee _{l\in L}t_i^l$$\end{document}⋁l∈Ltil ensures agents have at least one ToM order. Here, ToM orders indicate the maximum order an agent can use. Further details, as well as soundness and completeness, are left for future work.

In the present paper we opt to model ToM limitations only, even though working memory limitations, which interact with ToM, are well-established (Miller, 1956). We also treat all perspective switches between different agents the same, even though the work of van Viegen (2014) shows that a sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_aB_bB_a$$\end{document}BaBbBa may be harder to interpret than a sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_aB_bB_c$$\end{document}BaBbBc. Such restrictions can be investigated by devising more involved axioms, or by integrating our logic with the work on depth-bounded reasoning (D’Agostino, 2015).

Computational cognitive models

While our modeling decisions are inspired by prior behavioural research, our logic can also make its own predictions which we aim to test with future behavioural experiments, using an approach similar to Zhang et al. (2021), Minculescu (2024), and Top et al. (2024). All three of these studies propose their own public announcement logic with ToM limitations, which is used at the core of a computational cognitive model of human reasoning. These models are then fit to experimental data: In Zhang et al. (2021) and Top et al. (2024) the game of Aces and Eights is used, while Minculescu (2024) lets participants play variations of Cheryl’s Birthday puzzle.

Going further, our logic could be used to create training agents similar to the ones employed in Veltman et al. (2019). Such agents may improve ToM use and help aid in deception detection.

Conclusion

In the present paper we present a sound and strongly complete dynamic epistemic logic with lying and theory of mind (ToM) limitations, which is inspired by prior behavioural research. As the example in Section 3.4.1 shows, our logic can be used to model how agents with bounded ToM can lie to each other. As stated in our introduction, our goal was to develop a logic that can model both lying announcements and human ToM limitations. In this paper this is exactly what we do, to our knowledge for the first time in the literature.
